Yeah, um… mostly because people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like my supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be saying something, and I’m looking at them, I swear I am, but then a minute later I realize I only caught maybe half of it.

And then I’m asking them to repeat themselves, and they get frustrated—my supervisor especially, he’s got this way of pausing like I should already know what he said. It’s not like I’m trying to ignore anybody. It just… slips by me, I guess. That’s the main thing that pushed me to come in.

It’s not like I’m trying to ignore anybody. It just… slips by me, I guess. Like if we’re in a safety huddle at work, I’ll be standing or sitting there and my leg’s going the whole time. My wife will be talking and I’m tapping or bouncing my knee, and sometimes people are like, “Man, you’re shaking the whole bench.” I don’t even notice until somebody points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. If I’ve got a pen or my keys, I’m messing with them without even thinking.\n\nSame at dinner, honestly. My wife will be talking and I’m tapping or bouncing my knee, and sometimes people are like, “Man, you’re shaking the whole bench.” I don’t even notice until somebody points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, not just talking over people, though that happens sometimes. At work it’s more like I jump into what someone else is doing before they ask me. Like if a coworker’s setting up a machine or something and I can see what I think is wrong, I’ll just reach in or start telling them, “No, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then, yeah, it turns into an argument because I didn’t wait, you know? I’m not trying to be a jerk, I just see it and I’m already in there before I think. Some guys really don’t like that, and honestly I get it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or when doing things with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly at work, though. Like if somebody’s setting something up and I’m waiting for them to finish or ask for help, I don’t really wait. Yeah… yeah, I do. On the line, especially, I’ll kind of rush through the quality checks because I’m trying to keep things moving, and then I miss dumb little stuff. Like a wrong label, or a fitting that wasn’t tightened all the way.

Honestly, I think the listening part is what got me in here more than anything. Like, yeah, I jump in on people, but people keep telling me I’m not listening.

I just… I don’t know, it doesn’t all stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on what's being said or with acting impulsively without thinking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the staying focused on what’s being said. I mean, I do act before thinking sometimes, sure, but what really brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking straight to me, or my wife will, and I’m looking right at them, and then a minute later I realize I only caught half of it. Then I’m asking them to say it again, and it’s… yeah, it frustrates everybody. My wife gets that look like, “I just told you this,” and she did, I just didn’t hold onto all of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Listening_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening—my supervisor or my wife will be talking straight to me, and a minute later I realize I only caught half of it and I’m asking them to say it again.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.

Like my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.

It’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.

I mean, I do act before thinking sometimes, sure, but what really brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking straight to me, or my wife will, and I’m looking right at them, and then a minute later I realize I only caught half of it. Then I’m asking them to say it again, and it’s… yeah, it frustrates everybody. My wife gets that look like, “I just told you this,” and she did, I just didn’t hold onto all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.\n\nIt’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your trouble with listening is because you're distracted by other things or because you just can't seem to focus on what the other person is saying?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Listening_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening—my supervisor or my wife will be talking straight to me, and a minute later I realize I only caught half of it and I’m asking them to say it again.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more like I just can’t stay locked onto what they’re saying. I mean, sometimes there’s stuff going on around me—at work there’s always noise, machines, people moving around—but even when it’s just my wife talking right to me, I’ll be looking at her and thinking I’m with her, and then… I don’t know, a minute later I’ve only got half of it.

That’s kind of what brought me in. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will explain something, or my wife will tell me something, and then I’m asking them to repeat it. And I hate doing that, because they get frustrated, like, “I just said that.” And they did. I just didn’t hang onto it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely not just work. I mean, work is where it causes the biggest headache because my supervisor will be telling me something straight to my face, and then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was the second part?” and I feel stupid asking again.

But it happens at home too, mostly with my wife. She’ll tell me something, and I’m looking right at her, and then somehow I only caught half of it. Then she gets frustrated because, you know, she did say it. I just didn’t hold onto it.

With friends or stuff I enjoy… I guess it can happen there too, but the main thing that pushed me to come in was people close to me saying I’m not listening. Like it’s become this whole thing.

At home, my wife and kids get frustrated because I disrupt routines instead of helping them run smoothly.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: It’s been this bad for about two years and two months, since my wife started working outside the home and I had to handle more of the schedule on my own.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S003\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.\n\nIt’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is because you're distracted by other things or because you just can't seem to focus on what the other person is saying?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I just can’t stay locked onto what they’re saying. I mean, sometimes there’s stuff going on around me—at work there’s always noise, machines, people moving around—but even when it’s just my wife talking right to me, I’ll be looking at her and thinking I’m with her, and then… I don’t know, a minute later I’ve only got half of it.\n\nThat’s kind of what brought me in. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will explain something, or my wife will tell me something, and then I’m asking them to repeat it. And I hate doing that, because they get frustrated, like, “I just said that.” And they did. I just didn’t hang onto it.\n\nDoctor: Does your trouble with listening happen just at work or also when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work. I mean, work is where it causes the biggest headache because my supervisor will be telling me something straight to my face, and then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was the second part?” and I feel stupid asking again.\n\nBut it happens at home too, mostly with my wife. She’ll tell me something, and I’m looking right at her, and then somehow I only caught half of it. Then she gets frustrated because, you know, she did say it. I just didn’t hold onto it.\n\nWith friends or stuff I enjoy… I guess it can happen there too, but the main thing that pushed me to come in was people close to me saying I’m not listening. Like it’s become this whole thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your trouble with listening is more because you're not paying attention or because your mind is already moving on to something else?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess it’s more like I’m not staying with it, if that makes sense. Like they’ll be talking right to me—my supervisor, or my wife—and I’m there, I’m looking at them, but then a minute later I realize I only got half of what they said.

And then I’m asking them, “Wait, can you say that again?” which… that’s really what brought me in, honestly. People keep saying I’m not listening. My wife especially gets frustrated, and I get it, because she did tell me. I just didn’t seem to catch the whole thing.

I don’t always know if my mind is moving on to something else or if it just drops out, you know? It’s like I’m there, but I’m not fully taking it in.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… mostly because people keep telling me I’m not listening. It’s not like I’m trying to ignore anybody. It just… slips by me, I guess. But I don’t think it’s just them. Like, someone can be talking right at me, and I’m nodding, and then it’s like… wait, what did they just say?\n\nMy supervisor gets irritated because I’ll ask him to repeat it, and my wife too—she’ll say, “You weren’t listening,” and I swear I was trying to. I just only catch half of it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about other things when people are talking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes I guess, but it’s hard to say what I’m thinking about because half the time I don’t even notice I drifted. Like my supervisor will be talking right to me on the floor, and I’m looking at him, nodding, and then a minute later I’m like, wait… I only got part of that.\n\nSame thing with my wife. She’ll be telling me something, and then I have to ask her to say it again, and she gets irritated. It’s not like I’m off thinking, “Oh, I need to do this other thing,” every time. It just kind of… slips past me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still when you're in a meeting or a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like if we’re in a safety huddle at work, I’ll be standing or sitting there and my leg’s going the whole time. If I’ve got a pen or my keys, I’m messing with them without even thinking.\n\nSame at dinner, honestly. My wife will be talking and I’m tapping or bouncing my knee, and sometimes people are like, “Man, you’re shaking the whole bench.” I don’t even notice until somebody points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, not just talking over people, though that happens sometimes. At work it’s more like I jump into what someone else is doing before they ask me. Like if a coworker’s setting up a machine or something and I can see what I think is wrong, I’ll just reach in or start telling them, “No, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then, yeah, it turns into an argument because I didn’t wait, you know? I’m not trying to be a jerk, I just see it and I’m already in there before I think. Some guys really don’t like that, and honestly I get it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or when doing things with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly at work, though. Like if somebody’s setting something up and I’m waiting for them to finish or ask for help, I don’t really wait. I’ll just step in, reach over, start fixing it, or tell them, “No, no, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then it turns into a whole thing because, I mean, nobody likes somebody jumping into their job. I know that. It’s just in the moment I see what I think needs doing and I’m already doing it before I’ve really… I don’t know, before it’s my turn, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. On the line, especially, I’ll kind of rush through the quality checks because I’m trying to keep things moving, and then I miss dumb little stuff. Like a wrong label, or a fitting that wasn’t tightened all the way.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t know how to do the check. I’ve done that kind of work forever. But then my supervisor has to pull me aside like, “Hey, this happened again,” and I’m standing there feeling stupid, because it’s small stuff I should’ve caught. Sometimes I’m already thinking about the next part of the job, or somebody’s asking something nearby, and then—yeah, I just blow right past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble paying attention to what's being said or waiting for your turn to speak or act?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think the listening part is what got me in here more than anything. Like, yeah, I jump in on people, but people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nMy supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I’m looking at them like I’m hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I only caught half of what they said. Then I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say again?” and that gets old for people. My wife especially—she’ll say, “I just told you this,” and she’s not wrong. I just… I don’t know, it doesn’t all stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on what's being said or with acting impulsively without thinking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the staying focused on what’s being said. I mean, I do act before thinking sometimes, sure, but what really brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking straight to me, or my wife will, and I’m looking right at them, and then a minute later I realize I only caught half of it. Then I’m asking them to say it again, and it’s… yeah, it frustrates everybody. My wife gets that look like, “I just told you this,” and she did, I just didn’t hold onto all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. I mean, not just talking over people, though that happens sometimes. At work it’s more like I jump into what someone else is doing before they ask me. Like if a coworker’s setting up a machine or something and I can see what I think is wrong, I’ll just reach in or start telling them, “No, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then, yeah, it turns into an argument because I didn’t wait, you know? I’m not trying to be a jerk, I just see it and I’m already in there before I think. Some guys really don’t like that, and honestly I get it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or when doing things with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly at work, though. Like if somebody’s setting something up and I’m waiting for them to finish or ask for help, I don’t really wait. I’ll just step in, reach over, start fixing it, or tell them, “No, no, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then it turns into a whole thing because, I mean, nobody likes somebody jumping into their job. I know that. It’s just in the moment I see what I think needs doing and I’m already doing it before I’ve really… I don’t know, before it’s my turn, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. On the line, especially, I’ll kind of rush through the quality checks because I’m trying to keep things moving, and then I miss dumb little stuff. Like a wrong label, or a fitting that wasn’t tightened all the way.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t know how to do the check. I’ve done that kind of work forever. But then my supervisor has to pull me aside like, “Hey, this happened again,” and I’m standing there feeling stupid, because it’s small stuff I should’ve caught. Sometimes I’m already thinking about the next part of the job, or somebody’s asking something nearby, and then—yeah, I just blow right past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble paying attention to what's being said or waiting for your turn to speak or act?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think the listening part is what got me in here more than anything. Like, yeah, I jump in on people, but people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nMy supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I’m looking at them like I’m hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I only caught half of what they said. Then I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say again?” and that gets old for people. My wife especially—she’ll say, “I just told you this,” and she’s not wrong. I just… I don’t know, it doesn’t all stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on what's being said or with acting impulsively without thinking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the staying focused on what’s being said. I mean, I do act before thinking sometimes, sure, but what really brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking straight to me, or my wife will, and I’m looking right at them, and then a minute later I realize I only caught half of it. Then I’m asking them to say it again, and it’s… yeah, it frustrates everybody. My wife gets that look like, “I just told you this,” and she did, I just didn’t hold onto all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.\n\nIt’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is because you're distracted by other things or because you just can't seem to focus on what the other person is saying?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I just can’t stay locked onto what they’re saying. I mean, sometimes there’s stuff going on around me—at work there’s always noise, machines, people moving around—but even when it’s just my wife talking right to me, I’ll be looking at her and thinking I’m with her, and then… I don’t know, a minute later I’ve only got half of it.\n\nThat’s kind of what brought me in. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will explain something, or my wife will tell me something, and then I’m asking them to repeat it. And I hate doing that, because they get frustrated, like, “I just said that.” And they did. I just didn’t hang onto it.\n\nDoctor: Does your trouble with listening happen just at work or also when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work. I mean, work is where it causes the biggest headache because my supervisor will be telling me something straight to my face, and then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was the second part?” and I feel stupid asking again.\n\nBut it happens at home too, mostly with my wife. She’ll tell me something, and I’m looking right at her, and then somehow I only caught half of it. Then she gets frustrated because, you know, she did say it. I just didn’t hold onto it.\n\nWith friends or stuff I enjoy… I guess it can happen there too, but the main thing that pushed me to come in was people close to me saying I’m not listening. Like it’s become this whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is more because you're not paying attention or because your mind is already moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more like I’m not staying with it, if that makes sense. Like they’ll be talking right to me—my supervisor, or my wife—and I’m there, I’m looking at them, but then a minute later I realize I only got half of what they said.\n\nAnd then I’m asking them, “Wait, can you say that again?” which… that’s really what brought me in, honestly. People keep saying I’m not listening. My wife especially gets frustrated, and I get it, because she did tell me. I just didn’t seem to catch the whole thing.\n\nI don’t always know if my mind is moving on to something else or if it just drops out, you know? It’s like I’m there, but I’m not fully taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming when you're supposed to be paying attention to something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it daydreaming exactly. It’s more like someone’s talking to me and I’m supposed to be taking it in, and then suddenly I realize I only got half of it.\n\nLike my supervisor will be explaining something right in front of me, or my wife will be telling me something at home, and I’m looking at them, but a minute later I’m asking, “Wait, can you say that again?” And then they’re annoyed because they did just say it. That’s really what made me come in, people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening and acting impulsively is happening at the same time, or is one happening more often than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the listening thing is the one I notice more, or at least it’s the thing people keep calling me out on. Like, my supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I swear I’m trying to listen, but then a minute later I only caught half of it.\n\nThen I’m asking, “Wait, say that again?” and that turns into a whole thing. My wife especially gets irritated, which I understand. So… yeah, I don’t know if it’s exactly happening at the same time as acting impulsively, but the not-listening part is what really brought me in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just didn’t seem to catch the whole thing.\n\nI don’t always know if my mind is moving on to something else or if it just drops out, you know? It’s like I’m there, but I’m not fully taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming when you're supposed to be paying attention to something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it daydreaming exactly. It’s more like someone’s talking to me and I’m supposed to be taking it in, and then suddenly I realize I only got half of it.\n\nLike my supervisor will be explaining something right in front of me, or my wife will be telling me something at home, and I’m looking at them, but a minute later I’m asking, “Wait, can you say that again?” And then they’re annoyed because they did just say it. That’s really what made me come in, people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening and acting impulsively is happening at the same time, or is one happening more often than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the listening thing is the one I notice more, or at least it’s the thing people keep calling me out on. Like, my supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I swear I’m trying to listen, but then a minute later I only caught half of it.\n\nThen I’m asking, “Wait, say that again?” and that turns into a whole thing. My wife especially gets irritated, which I understand. So… yeah, I don’t know if it’s exactly happening at the same time as acting impulsively, but the not-listening part is what really brought me in.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you act impulsively more often than you have trouble listening to others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Interrupting_Intruding\nmanifestation: \"I cut into coworkers’ jobs without being asked, like reaching in to fix their setup or telling them how to do it, and it causes arguments because I don’t wait my turn.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Probably… yeah, I think the impulsive stuff happens a lot too. Like at work, I’ll jump into someone else’s job without them asking me. If I see their setup looks wrong, I’ll just reach in and start fixing it, or I’ll start telling them, “No, do it this way,” before they even ask.

And then it turns into an argument because, you know, they’re like, “Back off, I had it,” and maybe they did. I just don’t wait my turn. I see it and I’m already moving, and then afterward I’m like, why did I do that? I should’ve just let them handle it or asked first.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… mostly because people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like my supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be saying something, and I’m looking at them, I swear I am, but then a minute later I realize I only caught maybe half of it.\n\nAnd then I’m asking them to repeat themselves, and they get frustrated—my supervisor especially, he’s got this way of pausing like I should already know what he said. It’s not like I’m trying to ignore anybody. It just… slips by me, I guess. That’s the main thing that pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen with everyone you talk to, or is it more with certain people like your supervisor or your wife?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the people I’m around a lot, I guess—my supervisor and my wife are the big ones because they’re the ones who call me out on it. But I don’t think it’s just them. Like, someone can be talking right at me, and I’m nodding, and then it’s like… wait, what did they just say?\n\nMy supervisor gets irritated because I’ll ask him to repeat it, and my wife too—she’ll say, “You weren’t listening,” and I swear I was trying to. I just only catch half of it sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about other things when people are talking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes I guess, but it’s hard to say what I’m thinking about because half the time I don’t even notice I drifted. Like my supervisor will be talking right to me on the floor, and I’m looking at him, nodding, and then a minute later I’m like, wait… I only got part of that.\n\nSame thing with my wife. She’ll be telling me something, and then I have to ask her to say it again, and she gets irritated. It’s not like I’m off thinking, “Oh, I need to do this other thing,” every time. It just kind of… slips past me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still when you're in a meeting or a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like if we’re in a safety huddle at work, I’ll be standing or sitting there and my leg’s going the whole time. If I’ve got a pen or my keys, I’m messing with them without even thinking.\n\nSame at dinner, honestly. My wife will be talking and I’m tapping or bouncing my knee, and sometimes people are like, “Man, you’re shaking the whole bench.” I don’t even notice until somebody points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, not just talking over people, though that happens sometimes. At work it’s more like I jump into what someone else is doing before they ask me. Like if a coworker’s setting up a machine or something and I can see what I think is wrong, I’ll just reach in or start telling them, “No, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then, yeah, it turns into an argument because I didn’t wait, you know? I’m not trying to be a jerk, I just see it and I’m already in there before I think. Some guys really don’t like that, and honestly I get it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or when doing things with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly at work, though. Like if somebody’s setting something up and I’m waiting for them to finish or ask for help, I don’t really wait. I’ll just step in, reach over, start fixing it, or tell them, “No, no, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then it turns into a whole thing because, I mean, nobody likes somebody jumping into their job. I know that. It’s just in the moment I see what I think needs doing and I’m already doing it before I’ve really… I don’t know, before it’s my turn, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. On the line, especially, I’ll kind of rush through the quality checks because I’m trying to keep things moving, and then I miss dumb little stuff. Like a wrong label, or a fitting that wasn’t tightened all the way.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t know how to do the check. I’ve done that kind of work forever. But then my supervisor has to pull me aside like, “Hey, this happened again,” and I’m standing there feeling stupid, because it’s small stuff I should’ve caught. Sometimes I’m already thinking about the next part of the job, or somebody’s asking something nearby, and then—yeah, I just blow right past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble paying attention to what's being said or waiting for your turn to speak or act?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think the listening part is what got me in here more than anything. Like, yeah, I jump in on people, but people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nMy supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I’m looking at them like I’m hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I only caught half of what they said. Then I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say again?” and that gets old for people. My wife especially—she’ll say, “I just told you this,” and she’s not wrong. I just… I don’t know, it doesn’t all stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on what's being said or with acting impulsively without thinking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the staying focused on what’s being said. I mean, I do act before thinking sometimes, sure, but what really brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking straight to me, or my wife will, and I’m looking right at them, and then a minute later I realize I only caught half of it. Then I’m asking them to say it again, and it’s… yeah, it frustrates everybody. My wife gets that look like, “I just told you this,” and she did, I just didn’t hold onto all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.\n\nIt’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is because you're distracted by other things or because you just can't seem to focus on what the other person is saying?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I just can’t stay locked onto what they’re saying. I mean, sometimes there’s stuff going on around me—at work there’s always noise, machines, people moving around—but even when it’s just my wife talking right to me, I’ll be looking at her and thinking I’m with her, and then… I don’t know, a minute later I’ve only got half of it.\n\nThat’s kind of what brought me in. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will explain something, or my wife will tell me something, and then I’m asking them to repeat it. I just didn’t hang onto it.\n\nDoctor: Does your trouble with listening happen just at work or also when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work. She’ll tell me something, and I’m looking right at her, and then somehow I only caught half of it. Then she gets frustrated because, you know, she did say it. I just didn’t hold onto it.\n\nWith friends or stuff I enjoy… I guess it can happen there too, but the main thing that pushed me to come in was people close to me saying I’m not listening. Like it’s become this whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is more because you're not paying attention or because your mind is already moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more like I’m not staying with it, if that makes sense. My wife will be talking and I’m tapping or bouncing my knee, and sometimes people are like, “Man, you’re shaking the whole bench.” I don’t even notice until somebody points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, not just talking over people, though that happens sometimes. At work it’s more like I jump into what someone else is doing before they ask me. Like if a coworker’s setting up a machine or something and I can see what I think is wrong, I’ll just reach in or start telling them, “No, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then, yeah, it turns into an argument because I didn’t wait, you know? I’m not trying to be a jerk, I just see it and I’m already in there before I think. Some guys really don’t like that, and honestly I get it after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or when doing things with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly at work, though. Like if somebody’s setting something up and I’m waiting for them to finish or ask for help, I don’t really wait. I’ll just step in, reach over, start fixing it, or tell them, “No, no, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then it turns into a whole thing because, I mean, nobody likes somebody jumping into their job. I know that. It’s just in the moment I see what I think needs doing and I’m already doing it before I’ve really… I don’t know, before it’s my turn, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. On the line, especially, I’ll kind of rush through the quality checks because I’m trying to keep things moving, and then I miss dumb little stuff. Like a wrong label, or a fitting that wasn’t tightened all the way.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t know how to do the check. I’ve done that kind of work forever. But then my supervisor has to pull me aside like, “Hey, this happened again,” and I’m standing there feeling stupid, because it’s small stuff I should’ve caught. Sometimes I’m already thinking about the next part of the job, or somebody’s asking something nearby, and then—yeah, I just blow right past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble paying attention to what's being said or waiting for your turn to speak or act?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think the listening part is what got me in here more than anything. Like, yeah, I jump in on people, but people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nMy supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I’m looking at them like I’m hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I only caught half of what they said. Then I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say again?” and that gets old for people. My wife especially—she’ll say, “I just told you this,” and she’s not wrong. I just… I don’t know, it doesn’t all stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on what's being said or with acting impulsively without thinking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the staying focused on what’s being said. I mean, I do act before thinking sometimes, sure, but what really brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking straight to me, or my wife will, and I’m looking right at them, and then a minute later I realize I only caught half of it. Then I’m asking them to say it again, and it’s… yeah, it frustrates everybody. My wife gets that look like, “I just told you this,” and she did, I just didn’t hold onto all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.\n\nIt’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is because you're distracted by other things or because you just can't seem to focus on what the other person is saying?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I just can’t stay locked onto what they’re saying. I mean, sometimes there’s stuff going on around me—at work there’s always noise, machines, people moving around—but even when it’s just my wife talking right to me, I’ll be looking at her and thinking I’m with her, and then… I don’t know, a minute later I’ve only got half of it.\n\nThat’s kind of what brought me in. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will explain something, or my wife will tell me something, and then I’m asking them to repeat it. And I hate doing that, because they get frustrated, like, “I just said that.” And they did. I just didn’t hang onto it.\n\nDoctor: Does your trouble with listening happen just at work or also when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work. I mean, work is where it causes the biggest headache because my supervisor will be telling me something straight to my face, and then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was the second part?” and I feel stupid asking again.\n\nBut it happens at home too, mostly with my wife. She’ll tell me something, and I’m looking right at her, and then somehow I only caught half of it. Then she gets frustrated because, you know, she did say it. I just didn’t hold onto it.\n\nWith friends or stuff I enjoy… I guess it can happen there too, but the main thing that pushed me to come in was people close to me saying I’m not listening. Like it’s become this whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is more because you're not paying attention or because your mind is already moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more like I’m not staying with it, if that makes sense. Like they’ll be talking right to me—my supervisor, or my wife—and I’m there, I’m looking at them, but then a minute later I realize I only got half of what they said.\n\nAnd then I’m asking them, “Wait, can you say that again?” which… that’s really what brought me in, honestly. People keep saying I’m not listening. My wife especially gets frustrated, and I get it, because she did tell me. I just didn’t seem to catch the whole thing.\n\nI don’t always know if my mind is moving on to something else or if it just drops out, you know? It’s like I’m there, but I’m not fully taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming when you're supposed to be paying attention to something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it daydreaming exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say the impulse part causes more trouble day to day. The listening thing is frustrating, sure, but the stuff that really blows up is when I jump in before I think.

Like at work, if I see a coworker setting something up and it looks off to me, I’ll just reach in and start fixing it, or I’ll tell them, “No, do it this way,” before they even ask. And I know how that sounds—my dad used to do that too, actually, like he couldn’t watch anybody do something “wrong” without taking over. But with me it turns into arguments because I don’t wait my turn. I’m already in their space, messing with their job, and then they’re mad, and honestly I get why.

So yeah… probably acting on impulse. That’s the one that causes the bigger problems.

But then my supervisor has to pull me aside like, “Hey, this happened again,” and I’m standing there feeling stupid, because it’s small stuff I should’ve caught. Like, yeah, I jump in on people, but people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nMy supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I’m looking at them like I’m hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I only caught half of what they said. Then I’m asking, “Wait, what did you say again?” and that gets old for people. My wife especially—she’ll say, “I just told you this,” and she’s not wrong. I just… I don’t know, it doesn’t all stick the first time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with staying focused on what's being said or with acting impulsively without thinking?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the staying focused on what’s being said. I mean, I do act before thinking sometimes, sure, but what really brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking straight to me, or my wife will, and I’m looking right at them, and then a minute later I realize I only caught half of it. Then I’m asking them to say it again, and it’s… yeah, it frustrates everybody. My wife gets that look like, “I just told you this,” and she did, I just didn’t hold onto all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.\n\nIt’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is because you're distracted by other things or because you just can't seem to focus on what the other person is saying?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I just can’t stay locked onto what they’re saying. I mean, sometimes there’s stuff going on around me—at work there’s always noise, machines, people moving around—but even when it’s just my wife talking right to me, I’ll be looking at her and thinking I’m with her, and then… I don’t know, a minute later I’ve only got half of it.\n\nThat’s kind of what brought me in. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will explain something, or my wife will tell me something, and then I’m asking them to repeat it. And I hate doing that, because they get frustrated, like, “I just said that.” And they did. I just didn’t hang onto it.\n\nDoctor: Does your trouble with listening happen just at work or also when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work. I mean, work is where it causes the biggest headache because my supervisor will be telling me something straight to my face, and then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was the second part?” and I feel stupid asking again.\n\nBut it happens at home too, mostly with my wife. She’ll tell me something, and I’m looking right at her, and then somehow I only caught half of it. Then she gets frustrated because, you know, she did say it. I just didn’t hold onto it.\n\nWith friends or stuff I enjoy… I guess it can happen there too, but the main thing that pushed me to come in was people close to me saying I’m not listening. Like it’s become this whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is more because you're not paying attention or because your mind is already moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more like I’m not staying with it, if that makes sense. Like they’ll be talking right to me—my supervisor, or my wife—and I’m there, I’m looking at them, but then a minute later I realize I only got half of what they said.\n\nAnd then I’m asking them, “Wait, can you say that again?” which… that’s really what brought me in, honestly. People keep saying I’m not listening. My wife especially gets frustrated, and I get it, because she did tell me. I just didn’t seem to catch the whole thing.\n\nI don’t always know if my mind is moving on to something else or if it just drops out, you know? It’s like I’m there, but I’m not fully taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming when you're supposed to be paying attention to something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it daydreaming exactly. It’s more like someone’s talking to me and I’m supposed to be taking it in, and then suddenly I realize I only got half of it.\n\nLike my supervisor will be explaining something right in front of me, or my wife will be telling me something at home, and I’m looking at them, but a minute later I’m asking, “Wait, can you say that again?” And then they’re annoyed because they did just say it. That’s really what made me come in, people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening and acting impulsively is happening at the same time, or is one happening more often than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the listening thing is the one I notice more, or at least it’s the thing people keep calling me out on. Like, my supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I swear I’m trying to listen, but then a minute later I only caught half of it.\n\nThen I’m asking, “Wait, say that again?” and that turns into a whole thing. My wife especially gets irritated, which I understand. So… yeah, I don’t know if it’s exactly happening at the same time as acting impulsively, but the not-listening part is what really brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you act impulsively more often than you have trouble listening to others?\n\nPatient: Probably… yeah, I think the impulsive stuff happens a lot too. Like at work, I’ll jump into someone else’s job without them asking me. If I see their setup looks wrong, I’ll just reach in and start fixing it, or I’ll start telling them, “No, do it this way,” before they even ask.\n\nAnd then it turns into an argument because, you know, they’re like, “Back off, I had it,” and maybe they did. I just don’t wait my turn. I see it and I’m already moving, and then afterward I’m like, why did I do that? I should’ve just let them handle it or asked first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think acting impulsively is a bigger problem for you than not listening to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the not listening gets me in trouble at home, but the impulsive part, that’s what really blows up at work.\n\nLike I’ll see a coworker doing something and I just—before I even think about it—I’m reaching into their setup or telling them, “No, no, do it this way.” And I’m not trying to be a jerk, I’m thinking I’m helping, but then they get mad because I didn’t wait or ask. It turns into an argument.\n\nIt’s like when you see a machine starting to run wrong and you just react, you know? Except sometimes it’s not my machine, not my job, and I should’ve let them handle it. So yeah, that’s probably the bigger problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more problems in your daily life due to acting on impulse or due to not being able to focus on what others are saying?\n\nPatient: I’d say the impulse part causes more trouble day to day. The listening thing is frustrating, sure, but the stuff that really blows up is when I jump in before I think.\n\nLike at work, if I see a coworker setting something up and it looks off to me, I’ll just reach in and start fixing it, or I’ll tell them, “No, do it this way,” before they even ask. And I know how that sounds—my dad used to do that too, actually, like he couldn’t watch anybody do something “wrong” without taking over. But with me it turns into arguments because I don’t wait my turn. I’m already in their space, messing with their job, and then they’re mad, and honestly I get why.\n\nSo yeah… probably acting on impulse. That’s the one that causes the bigger problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More at work, I think. I mean, I can be like that at home too, but work is where it really turns into something.

Like if I see somebody setting up a machine or doing a job and it looks wrong to me, I’ll just cut in. I’ll reach over and start fixing it, or I’ll start telling them, “No, do it like this,” before they even asked me. And then they get mad, which… yeah, I get it, because it’s their job and I didn’t wait my turn.

It’s usually not me trying to take over, it’s just—my hands are already moving before my brain catches up. Then it becomes an argument on the floor. So yeah, mostly at work.

I mean, not just talking over people, though that happens sometimes. At work it’s more like I jump into what someone else is doing before they ask me. Like if a coworker’s setting up a machine or something and I can see what I think is wrong, I’ll just reach in or start telling them, “No, do it this way.”\n\nAnd then, yeah, it turns into an argument because I didn’t wait, you know? I’m not trying to be a jerk, I just see it and I’m already in there before I think. I mean, I do act before thinking sometimes, sure, but what really brought me in is people keep saying I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking straight to me, or my wife will, and I’m looking right at them, and then a minute later I realize I only caught half of it. Then I’m asking them to say it again, and it’s… yeah, it frustrates everybody. My wife gets that look like, “I just told you this,” and she did, I just didn’t hold onto all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention to what others are saying or doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s more the paying attention to what people are saying. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.\n\nIt’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is because you're distracted by other things or because you just can't seem to focus on what the other person is saying?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I just can’t stay locked onto what they’re saying. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will explain something, or my wife will tell me something, and then I’m asking them to repeat it. I just didn’t hang onto it.\n\nDoctor: Does your trouble with listening happen just at work or also when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work. She’ll tell me something, and I’m looking right at her, and then somehow I only caught half of it. Then she gets frustrated because, you know, she did say it. I just didn’t hold onto it.\n\nWith friends or stuff I enjoy… I guess it can happen there too, but the main thing that pushed me to come in was people close to me saying I’m not listening. Like it’s become this whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is more because you're not paying attention or because your mind is already moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more like I’m not staying with it, if that makes sense. Like they’ll be talking right to me—my supervisor, or my wife—and I’m there, I’m looking at them, but then a minute later I realize I only got half of what they said.\n\nAnd then I’m asking them, “Wait, can you say that again?” which… that’s really what brought me in, honestly. People keep saying I’m not listening. My wife especially gets frustrated, and I get it, because she did tell me. I just didn’t seem to catch the whole thing.\n\nI don’t always know if my mind is moving on to something else or if it just drops out, you know? It’s like I’m there, but I’m not fully taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming when you're supposed to be paying attention to something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it daydreaming exactly. I mean, I do sometimes jump ahead, but the thing that really got me here is people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nLike my supervisor will be talking right at me, or my wife will be explaining something, and I’m standing there, looking at them, thinking I’m following it… and then a minute later I realize I only caught, like, half of it. Then I have to ask them to repeat it, and that just makes everybody irritated.\n\nIt’s weird too, because sometimes I’ll remember some random thing from earlier, like what machine was making a noise or what someone had for lunch, but not the thing my wife just told me I needed to do. So yeah… mostly the listening part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is because you're distracted by other things or because you just can't seem to focus on what the other person is saying?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I just can’t stay locked onto what they’re saying. I mean, sometimes there’s stuff going on around me—at work there’s always noise, machines, people moving around—but even when it’s just my wife talking right to me, I’ll be looking at her and thinking I’m with her, and then… I don’t know, a minute later I’ve only got half of it.\n\nThat’s kind of what brought me in. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will explain something, or my wife will tell me something, and then I’m asking them to repeat it. And I hate doing that, because they get frustrated, like, “I just said that.” And they did. I just didn’t hang onto it.\n\nDoctor: Does your trouble with listening happen just at work or also when you're with friends or doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just work. I mean, work is where it causes the biggest headache because my supervisor will be telling me something straight to my face, and then a minute later I’m like, “Wait, what was the second part?” and I feel stupid asking again.\n\nBut it happens at home too, mostly with my wife. She’ll tell me something, and I’m looking right at her, and then somehow I only caught half of it. Then she gets frustrated because, you know, she did say it. I just didn’t hold onto it.\n\nWith friends or stuff I enjoy… I guess it can happen there too, but the main thing that pushed me to come in was people close to me saying I’m not listening. Like it’s become this whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening is more because you're not paying attention or because your mind is already moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more like I’m not staying with it, if that makes sense. Like they’ll be talking right to me—my supervisor, or my wife—and I’m there, I’m looking at them, but then a minute later I realize I only got half of what they said.\n\nAnd then I’m asking them, “Wait, can you say that again?” which… that’s really what brought me in, honestly. People keep saying I’m not listening. My wife especially gets frustrated, and I get it, because she did tell me. I just didn’t seem to catch the whole thing.\n\nI don’t always know if my mind is moving on to something else or if it just drops out, you know? It’s like I’m there, but I’m not fully taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming when you're supposed to be paying attention to something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it daydreaming exactly. It’s more like someone’s talking to me and I’m supposed to be taking it in, and then suddenly I realize I only got half of it.\n\nLike my supervisor will be explaining something right in front of me, or my wife will be telling me something at home, and I’m looking at them, but a minute later I’m asking, “Wait, can you say that again?” And then they’re annoyed because they did just say it. That’s really what made me come in, people keep telling me I’m not listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with listening and acting impulsively is happening at the same time, or is one happening more often than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the listening thing is the one I notice more, or at least it’s the thing people keep calling me out on. Like, my supervisor will be talking right to me, or my wife will be telling me something, and I swear I’m trying to listen, but then a minute later I only caught half of it.\n\nThen I’m asking, “Wait, say that again?” and that turns into a whole thing. My wife especially gets irritated, which I understand. So… yeah, I don’t know if it’s exactly happening at the same time as acting impulsively, but the not-listening part is what really brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you act impulsively more often than you have trouble listening to others?\n\nPatient: Probably… yeah, I think the impulsive stuff happens a lot too. Like at work, I’ll jump into someone else’s job without them asking me. If I see their setup looks wrong, I’ll just reach in and start fixing it, or I’ll start telling them, “No, do it this way,” before they even ask.\n\nAnd then it turns into an argument because, you know, they’re like, “Back off, I had it,” and maybe they did. I just don’t wait my turn. I see it and I’m already moving, and then afterward I’m like, why did I do that? I should’ve just let them handle it or asked first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think acting impulsively is a bigger problem for you than not listening to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, the not listening gets me in trouble at home, but the impulsive part, that’s what really blows up at work.\n\nLike I’ll see a coworker doing something and I just—before I even think about it—I’m reaching into their setup or telling them, “No, no, do it this way.” And I’m not trying to be a jerk, I’m thinking I’m helping, but then they get mad because I didn’t wait or ask. It turns into an argument.\n\nIt’s like when you see a machine starting to run wrong and you just react, you know? Except sometimes it’s not my machine, not my job, and I should’ve let them handle it. So yeah, that’s probably the bigger problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more problems in your daily life due to acting on impulse or due to not being able to focus on what others are saying?\n\nPatient: I’d say the impulse part causes more trouble day to day. The listening thing is frustrating, sure, but the stuff that really blows up is when I jump in before I think.\n\nLike at work, if I see a coworker setting something up and it looks off to me, I’ll just reach in and start fixing it, or I’ll tell them, “No, do it this way,” before they even ask. And I know how that sounds—my dad used to do that too, actually, like he couldn’t watch anybody do something “wrong” without taking over. I’m already in their space, messing with their job, and then they’re mad, and honestly I get why.\n\nSo yeah… probably acting on impulse. That’s the one that causes the bigger problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you act more impulsively at work or at home with your family and friends?\n\nPatient: More at work, I think. I mean, I can be like that at home too, but work is where it really turns into something.\n\nLike if I see somebody setting up a machine or doing a job and it looks wrong to me, I’ll just cut in. I’ll reach over and start fixing it, or I’ll start telling them, “No, do it like this,” before they even asked me. And then they get mad, which… yeah, I get it, because it’s their job and I didn’t wait my turn.\n\nIt’s usually not me trying to take over, it’s just—my hands are already moving before my brain catches up. Then it becomes an argument on the floor. So yeah, mostly at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The impulsivity, yeah. I mean, the listening thing is there, but what really causes problems is when I jump into somebody else’s work before I even stop myself.
